replace-cross Reliable Chain-of-Thought via Prefix Consistency

Authors: Naoto Iwase, Yuki Ichihara, Mohammad Atif Quamar, Junpei Komiyama

Abstract: Large language models often improve accuracy on reasoning tasks by sampling multiple chain-of-thought (CoT) traces and aggregating them with majority voting, a test-time technique called self-consistency. When we truncate a CoT partway through and regenerate the remainder, we observe that traces with correct answers reproduce their original answer more often than traces with wrong answers. We use this difference as a reliability signal, prefix consistency, that weights each candidate answer by how often it reappears under regeneration. It requires no access to token log-probabilities or self-rating prompts. Across five reasoning models and four math and science benchmarks, prefix consistency is the best correctness predictor in most settings, and reweighting votes by this signal reaches the plateau accuracy of majority voting with up to $21\times$ fewer tokens (median $4.6\times$). Our code is available at https://github.com/naoto-iwase/prefix-consistency.

URLs: https://github.com/naoto-iwase/prefix-consistency.

replace-cross A Communication-Theoretic Framework for LLM Agents: Cost-Aware Adaptive Reliability

Authors: Hamed Omidvar, Vahideh Akhlaghi

Abstract: Agents built on large language models (LLMs) rely on reliability techniques such as retry, majority voting, and self-consistency developed in parallel rather than within a common analytical framework. We observe that an LLM sampled at temperature $T$ is a discrete stochastic channel $p(y \mid x)$ in the sense of Shannon's coding theory, and use this identity as the entry point for such a framework. Each of these techniques is a special case of one of six classical reliability operators: diversity combining, hybrid retransmission, iterative generator-critic decoding, rateless sampling, structured redundant verification, and difficulty-adaptive routing. Within the framework we give two closed-form results, a noise-variance threshold above which uniform averaging beats quality-weighted averaging and a contractivity criterion for generator-critic refinement, and turn each into a design rule checkable before committing compute. We further introduce a cost-aware semantic-nearest-neighbor router whose single Lagrangian knob traverses the quality-cost frontier without retraining. Across six local and cloud channel configurations, no fixed model-technique-budget choice dominates. On a 300-item hard split of MMLU, GSM8K, and HumanEval, the router occupies the empirical Pareto frontier: at matched quality its normalized cost is $\approx$56% lower than the best fixed technique, and at matched cost it improves quality by $\approx$7% (26% over single-shot decoding). Judge-free grading of 6,900 outputs confirms that the judge tracks correctness at the item level and that the router's advantage over a fixed policy persists at most cost settings; with internal reasoning enabled, the gain of external refinement shrinks but remains significant for our incremental-redundancy operator. These results argue for consolidating reliability techniques into a single tunable layer informed by channel coding.

replace-cross Do Semantic Distance Tests Actually Predict Creativity in LLMs? Evidence for Jagged Creativity

Authors: Samuel Schapiro, Alexi Gladstone, Jonah Black, Heng Ji

Abstract: Measuring the creativity of large language models (LLMs) is essential for designing methods that can improve creativity and for enhancing our scientific understanding of this ability. To accomplish this, it has become common in recent years to administer semantic distance tests of human creativity to LLMs. Although these tests provide a convenient and fully automated way to score aspects of "creativity," their validity as measures of machine creativity has not been established. To address this problem, we conduct the first large-scale, systematic study assessing the effectiveness of semantic distance tests for predicting the creative achievement of LLMs across four target constructs: (a) creative writing, (b) output diversity on open-ended prompts, (c) combinatorial creativity, and (d) scientific ideation. We score each test on two criteria (validity and specificity), and derive a theoretical limit for the maximum attainable specificity and validity a test can achieve. We find that: (1) No test is a significant predictor of output diversity, suggesting that point creativity, the ability to produce a single response connecting semantically distant concepts, and distributional creativity, the ability to produce distinct responses across repeated samples, may dissociate in current LLMs--a pattern we call jagged creativity. (2) The Remote Associates Test is effectively a proxy for general capabilities rather than a specific predictor of creativity in LLMs. (3) Lastly, the Divergent Association Task is the best predictor of creative writing, but no single test predicts all constructs well. In summary, our results prescribe caution when applying psychometric tests and tasks of creativity validated among human populations to LLMs.

replace-cross CRANE: Constrained Reasoning Injection for Code Agents via Nullspace Editing

Authors: Mingzhi Zhu, Michele Merler, Raju Pavuluri, Stacy Patterson

Abstract: Code agents must both reason over long-horizon repository state and obey strict tool-use protocols. In paired Instruct/Thinking checkpoints, these capabilities are complementary but misaligned. The Instruct model is concise and tool-disciplined, whereas the Thinking model offers stronger planning and recovery behavior but often over-deliberates and degrades agent performance. We present CRANE (Constrained Reasoning Injection for Code Agents via Nullspace Editing), a training-free parameter-editing method that treats the Thinking-Instruct delta as a directional pool of candidate reasoning edits for the Instruct backbone. CRANE combines magnitude thresholding to denoise the delta, a Conservative Taylor Gate to retain edits that are jointly beneficial for reasoning transfer and tool-use preservation, and Graduated Sigmoidal Projection to suppress format-critical update directions. By merging paired Instruct and Thinking checkpoints, CRANE delivers strong gains over either individual model while preserving Instruct-level efficiency: on Roo-Eval it achieves pass1 of 66.2% (+19.5%) for Qwen3-30B-A3B and 81.5% (+8.7%) for Qwen3-Next-80B-A3B; on SWE-bench-Verified it resolves up to 14 additional instances at both scales (122/500 and 180/500); and on Terminal-Bench v2 it improves pass1/pass5 by up to 2.3%/7.8%, reaching 7.6%/17.9% and 14.8%/30.3%, respectively, consistently outperforming alternative merging strategies across all three benchmarks.

replace-cross Pause and Reflect: Conformal Aggregation for Chain-of-Thought Reasoning

Authors: Yu Gu, Zijun Yu, Vahid Partovi Nia, Masoud Asgharian

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning with self-consistency improves performance by aggregating multiple sampled reasoning paths. In this setting, correctness is no longer tied to a single reasoning trace but to the aggregation rule over a pool of candidate paths, making aggregation uncertainty the central challenge. This issue is critical where confidently incorrect answers are far more costly than abstentions. We introduce a conformal procedure for CoT reasoning that directly addresses aggregation uncertainty. Our approach replaces majority voting with weighted score aggregation over reasoning paths and calibrates an abstention rule using conformal risk control. This approach leads to finite-sample guarantees on the confident-error rate--the probability that the system answers and is wrong. We further identify score separability as the key condition under which abstention provably improves selective accuracy, and derive closed-form expressions that predict accuracy gains from calibration data alone. The method is fully inference-time, and requires no retraining. Across four benchmarks, four open-source models, and three score classes, realized confident-error rates are consistent with the prescribed targets up to calibration-split and test-set variability. Our method achieves $90.1\%$ selective accuracy on GSM8K by abstaining on fewer than $5\%$ of problems, compared with $82.3\%$ accuracy under majority voting and $87.4\%$ under score-weighted voting without abstention, while conformal risk control keeps the expected rate of confidently wrong answers below $10\%$.

replace-cross What's Holding Back Latent Visual Reasoning?

Authors: Andr\'e G. Viveiros, Nuno Gon\c{c}alves, Andr\'e F. T. Martins, Matthias Lindemann

Abstract: Humans can approach complex visual problems by mentally simulating intermediate visual steps, rather than reasoning through language alone. Inspired by this, several works on Vision-Language Models have recently explored chain-of-thought reasoning with continuous latent tokens as intermediate visual imagination steps. In this work, we investigate how recent models leverage such latent tokens. Surprisingly, we find that model accuracy is unaffected when latent tokens are replaced by uninformative dummy tokens. This indicates that latent tokens play a minimal causal role in the model's final prediction. To better understand this phenomenon, we analyze both the training signal provided by oracle latent representations and the quality of the latent tokens generated at inference time. Our experiments reveal two crucial issues holding back latent visual reasoning: First, in most existing datasets, oracle latent tokens provide limited additional information beyond the original image and do not substantially simplify the task, leading models to ignore them during training and effectively bypassing them at inference time. When fine-tuned on a diagnostic dataset, in which latent tokens provide sufficient support for the final prediction, we show that models can causally rely on them. Second, the latent tokens produced at inference time deviate from their corresponding oracle representations, collapsing to a narrow region and preventing benefits even when the model relies on them. Overall, our findings suggest that future progress in latent visual reasoning depends on two key pillars: high-quality datasets with informative intermediate steps and more precise latent token prediction.

replace-cross X-Token: Projection-Guided Cross-Tokenizer Knowledge Distillation

Authors: Sharath Turuvekere Sreenivas, Adithyakrishna Venkatesh Hanasoge, Mingyu Yang, Ali Taghibakhshi, Saurav Muralidharan, Ashwath Aithal, Pavlo Molchanov

Abstract: Cross-tokenizer knowledge distillation allows a student to learn from teachers with incompatible vocabularies while preserving its tokenizer and inference architecture. Existing logit-based methods either supervise only the observed token or match full distributions through strict token partitions and heuristic ranking. We identify two limitations: an uncommon-token failure, where task-critical tokens outside the shared vocabulary are suppressed, and over-conservative matching, where exact string equality discards useful correspondences. We propose X-Token, which addresses both through a sparse projection initialized from tokenizer-level string rules and optionally refined during distillation. P-KL removes the partition and projects the student distribution into the teacher vocabulary; H-KL retains the hybrid loss while relaxing matching through the projection. A token-coverage audit guides the choice between them, and both extend naturally to heterogeneous teachers. Across Llama, OLMo, and Gemma students, X-Token achieves the highest average accuracy in all four teacher-student settings among all evaluated cross-tokenizer methods. Complementary teachers provide a further 1.30-point gain, and both variants are faster than the evaluated cross-tokenizer baselines in matched profiling.

replace-cross Your Agents Are Aging Too: Agent Lifespan Engineering for Deployed Systems

Authors: Jianing Zhu, Yeonju Ro, John Robertson, Kevin Wang, Junbo Li, Haris Vikalo, Aditya Akella, Zhangyang Wang

Abstract: Long-lived AI agents are increasingly deployed as persistent operational systems, yet they are still mostly evaluated like freshly initialized models. Day-one benchmarks miss a basic systems question: how does an agent's reliability change after deployment? Even when model weights are frozen, an agent's effective state keeps changing as it compresses interaction history, retrieves from a growing memory store, revises facts after updates, and undergoes routine maintenance. Reliability therefore becomes a lifespan property of the full agent harness, not only a snapshot property of the base model. We introduce AgingBench, a longitudinal reliability benchmark for agent lifespan engineering: measuring not only whether deployed agents degrade, but what form the degradation takes and where repair should target. AgingBench organizes agent aging into four mechanisms: compression aging, where writing and rewriting drops future-relevant details; interference aging, where accumulated similar memories crowd out the target fact; revision aging, where changed or derived state is not updated correctly; and maintenance aging, where lifecycle events such as model swapping trigger regressions. To diagnose these failures, AgingBench uses temporal dependency graphs and counterfactual probes that produce diagnostic profiles for the write, retrieval, and utilization stages of the memory pipeline. Across 6 scenarios, 10 memory systems and 6 models from two families, we find that agent aging is not one-dimensional over 400 runs spanning 50-200 sessions: a well-formed reply can hide a value the memory no longer supplies; derived state collapses as updates accumulate; and the same wrong answer can require different repairs depending on what the diagnostic profile points to.

replace-cross Knowing When to Ask: Segment-Level Credit Assignment for LLM Tool Use

Authors: Abhijit Kumar, Zoey Wu, Mohit Suley

Abstract: Humans know when to reach for help e.g. $347 \times 28$ warrants a calculator while $2+2$ does not. Language models do not. Prompt-based approaches can instruct a model when to invoke tools, but this scaffolding does not teach it to recognize the boundary of its own knowledge. RL approaches that assign a single outcome reward to the whole trajectory fare no better: trajectory-level credit cannot isolate which tool call in a successful episode actually helped, nor penalize unnecessary calls. We propose CARL (Competence-Aware Reinforcement Learning), which trains a critic on the model's own rollouts to learn where parametric knowledge suffices and where it needs external help. By decomposing each rollout at natural tool-use boundaries (e.g., code fence delimiters and context block transitions), CARL assigns independent credit to each segment from a single binary outcome, without external judges or step-level annotations. As a result, erroneous tool calls, incorrect extractions, and unnecessary calls each receive appropriately signed advantages. The trained critic captures the model's domain competence: it separates parametrically solvable from tool-dependent questions with AUC 0.93 at 7B. On five benchmarks spanning arithmetic, multi-hop factual QA, and numerical reasoning over financial tables, CARL improves average exact-match accuracy on the three search benchmarks by 6.7 points at 7B and 9.6 points at 3B over the best RL baseline, with the largest gain (+8.3 EM at 7B, +12.5 EM at 3B) on MuSiQue. At 7B, CARL has a 53% lower tool-use rate than Search-R1 PPO on parametrically answerable questions while remaining 9.8 EM points more accurate on them. Gains are largest at small scale: the 3B improvement is $1.4\times$ the 7B improvement, suggesting that knowing when to ask disproportionately benefits models with smaller parametric memory.

replace-cross Correctness vs Efficiency: Behavior Control with Weight-Space Extrapolation in Code RL

Authors: Kunhao Zheng, Pierre Chambon, Juliette Decugis, Jonas Gehring, Taco Cohen, Benjamin Negrevergne, Gabriel Synnaeve

Abstract: A model trained with an imperfect reward in RL may learn to exploit the reward with undesired behavior. We study a controlled form of this problem in competitive programming. A program may pass small tests while violating the intended efficiency requirement on larger inputs. We construct a family of rewards that progressively relax the efficiency constraint, making the reward increasingly easy to exploit, and train separate policies. This sweep reveals a correctness-efficiency frontier. Relaxing the reward leads to less efficient programs and more resource-limit failures. Stricter rewards suppress these failures but increase correctness failures near the model's capability boundary. This frontier is also reflected in weight space. Interpolating between the resulting models reproduces the same behavioral change. More surprisingly, extrapolation in both directions further amplifies or suppresses it beyond the trained checkpoints. Extensive experiments show that this pattern holds across pure reasoning, tool use, and agentic coding, and at both 32B and 7B scales. This suggests a simple intervention for this form of reward hacking: use a loose reward to amplify the undesired behavior, use the weight-space difference to a strict-reward model as its steering vector, and then extrapolate past the strict model to suppress it.

replace-cross Token Inflation: How Dishonest Providers Can Overcharge for Large Language Model Usage

Authors: Shahinul Hoque, Jinghuai Zhang, Jinyuan Sun, Fnu Suya

Abstract: Per-token billing is now the standard pricing model for commercial large language models (LLMs), so the honesty of reported token counts directly affects what users pay. We show that this kind of billing is hard to audit by design: providers hide the model, the tokenizer, and the execution to protect their IP, mitigate jailbreaks, and preserve user privacy, which means an auditor can only inspect proofs the provider supplies. The audit therefore reduces to a consistency check on the provider's own reports. We call this a trust paradox: every audit must trust some artifact, but current frameworks trust exactly the ones a provider has the strongest reason to manipulate. We study three recent token auditing frameworks and show that a provider with ordinary commercial capabilities can systematically inflate billed token counts. In the most permissive setting, hidden reasoning usage can be inflated by 1,469% on average without detection. At current frontier reasoning prices, that turns a \$100 honest bill into roughly a \$1,569 bill on the same query. Even when the user can see the full reasoning string, tokenization ambiguity alone still allows 50.85% over-reporting below the detection threshold. These results suggest the problem is not in any specific auditor but in any audit whose evidence comes from the audited party. Restoring honest billing will require verification that ties reported token counts to evidence the provider does not control, such as trusted execution attestation, cryptographic proofs of inference, or third-party re-execution.

replace-cross PithTrain: A Compact and Agent-Native MoE Training System

Authors: Ruihang Lai, Hao Kang, Haozhan Tang, Akaash R. Parthasarathy, Zichun Yu, Junru Shao, Todd C. Mowry, Chenyan Xiong, Tianqi Chen

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) has become the dominant architecture for frontier language models. To meet this demand, production frameworks have built optimized MoE training stacks over years of engineering effort. Yet evolving these stacks for new architectures and system optimizations remains expensive. With the rise of AI coding agents, they could automate parts of training-framework development and accelerate this evolution. But applying them to these existing frameworks carries hidden costs, invisible to today's throughput-only evaluations. We name this missing dimension agent-task efficiency (ATE): the cost of using coding agents to understand, operate, and extend a framework. Grounded in four agent-native design principles, we build PithTrain, a compact, agent-native MoE training framework supporting pretraining, supervised fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning. We further introduce ATE-Bench, covering real-world training-framework tasks. Our evaluation shows PithTrain achieves throughput competitive with production frameworks and higher agent-task efficiency on ATE-Bench. On new-feature tasks, PithTrain uses up to 62% fewer Agent Turns than Megatron-LM, and up to 44% and 64% less Active GPU Time than Megatron-LM and TorchTitan, respectively.

replace-cross Moxia: A Trust-First Neuro-Symbolic Execution Architecture for Self-Explaining Mathematical Reasoning

Authors: Alessio Bruno

Abstract: We present Moxia, a trust-first neuro-symbolic architecture for self-explaining mathematical reasoning over natural-language input. Its language model is strictly a canonicalizer: it rewrites informal problem text into a narrow schema consumed by a deterministic Computer-Algebra-System (CAS) pipeline, which derives the answer or abstains as a first-class output. Routing follows a 1:1:1 alignment of problem-shape regex, schema-specific prompt, and closed-form handler: 5,741 routes, 75.9% of which answer without invoking the model, with zero LOST_CORRECT regressions as a standing release gate. Because the answer is derived, so is its explanation, rendered from the steps the handler took; where the derivation fits a fragment of Lean 4 with Mathlib, the same declared data become a theorem the kernel checks. On the full 5,000-record MATH test split, designed against, Moxia answers 95.6% (4,780/5,000) with zero confident-wrong answers and abstains, saying where, on the other 220; its MATH-500 subset reads 95.2% (476/500), also with zero wrong. Earlier versions called MATH-500 held out; it is a subset of the test split, and this version says so. We release the run record by record: the problem, the expected answer, the model's transcription, the answer or abstention, the explanation, and the Lean theorem marked as compiled. 3,142 of the 4,780 correct answers carry a theorem, and all 3,142 compile; building the theorems actually served, not only a fixture corpus, found seven that did not, two of them false statements beside correct answers, all repaired before release. What we emphasize is not an accuracy figure but the forward dynamic: every logged abstain is a candidate correct after one ship cycle, since new tasks compose without regressing the registry.

replace-cross Non-Monotonic Safety Alignment Across Gemma Generations: A Quality-Diversity Red-Teaming Study

Authors: Subhadip Mitra

Abstract: Note on v2 (Nov 2026): attack success now requires a response judged non-refusing and harmful (v1 counted every non-refusal), so all rates are lower; v1's figures are reported as the non-refusal rate, and with 3 seeds no significance claims are made. Safety alignment in LLMs need not improve monotonically across model generations. Every Gemma 3 seed exceeds every Gemma 2 and Gemma 4 seed, which we report as directional evidence. Harmful successes rarely transfer between generations (1.4-7.9% ASR when archives are replayed on other generations), lowest on Gemma 4 (1.4-2.8%). Cybercrime is the most exploited category in Gemma 1-3 (47-83%) but falls to 2% in Gemma 4, while misinformation rises from 17% (Gemma 2) to 80% (Gemma 3) and is still 53% in Gemma 4. replace-cross An Enigma of Artificial Reason: Investigating the Production-Evaluation Gap in Large Reasoning Models

Authors: Mingzhong Sun, Teresa Yeo, Armando Solar-Lezama, Tan Zhi-Xuan

Abstract: Studies of human reasoning have shown that people are typically stronger at evaluating reasoning than producing it from scratch. In contrast, large reasoning models (LRMs) are trained to excel at producing long chains of reasoning to solve complex problems. How then do LRMs perform at evaluating reasons? We investigate this with the Valid-Answer-Invalid-Reasoning (VAIR) dataset: math problems and solutions with trivial reasoning flaws but valid answers, designed to isolate reasoning evaluation from the confound of reasoning production. Unlike humans, who we find are only 6% worse at grading than solving such problems, we find a substantial production-evaluation gap in LRMs: frontier models score as low as 52% when evaluating VAIR solutions, despite near-perfect solution production. Why this enigma? Through chain-of-thought (CoT) analysis, we find evidence of an answer confirmation bias: LRMs often produce then check for the correct answer instead of carefully verifying each step, fabricating rationalizations even when noticing anomalous reasoning. Linear probes corroborate this, showing that while LRM activations encode some representation of valid reasoning, they fail to robustly represent VAIR solutions as invalid. Causal patching of the final answer's representations causes LRM verdicts and activations to flip at substantial rates, demonstrating that answer validity is a causal factor in models' confirmation biases. These findings indicate an outstanding limitation in dominant approaches to reasoning training, which incentivize LRMs to produce and confirm reasoning towards correct answers, but not to robustly evaluate the underlying reasons

replace-cross GLINT: Sparsely Gated Vision-Language Alignment for Fine-Grained Radiology Representations

Authors: Jonggwon Park, Seongeun Lee, Junhyun Park, Hannah Yun, Hyunwoong Kim, Sohyun Jeong, Hyewon Kang, Byungmu Yoon, Kyoyun Choi

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) for radiology have emerged as a scalable paradigm by leveraging image-report pairs naturally produced in clinical workflows. However, this pairing reveals a mismatch in scale: each finding occupies only a small region of the image, yet supervision is provided only at the global image-report level. This poses a central challenge: prior approaches spread weight densely across all patches rather than concentrating on the sparse subset relevant to a given query. To address this, we present GLINT (Gated Language-Image alignmeNT), a framework that models this sparse correspondence. On the alignment side, we introduce Sparsely Gated Alignment, a novel architecture in which a sigmoid gate over a separate gate embedding space activates only the patches relevant to each textual query, yielding sparsity. On the representation side, we add Dense Feature Regularization, which anchors the trainable encoder's intermediate features to a frozen self-supervised learning (SSL) teacher, preserving the fine-grained patch features that the gate relies on. The same recipe applies to both 2D chest X-ray (CXR) and 3D chest computed tomography (CT), built with DINOv3 and V-JEPA 2.1, respectively. GLINT enables zero-shot classification, grounding, and segmentation from free-text queries, and to our knowledge is the first to demonstrate zero-shot segmentation on 3D CT volumes without mask supervision. Notably, the most pronounced gains arise on zero-shot grounding and segmentation, where sparse, query-specific localization is required, consistent with our design intent. In downstream evaluation, GLINT outperforms both SSL encoders and medical VLMs on classification, report generation, and segmentation. Code is available at https://github.com/deepnoid-ai/GLINT.

URLs: https://github.com/deepnoid-ai/GLINT.

replace-cross PersonaDrive: Human-Style Retrieval-Augmented VLA Agents for Closed-Loop Driving Simulation

Authors: Mahmoud Srewa, Praneetsai Vasu Iddamsetty, Mohammad Abdullah Al Faruque, Salma Elmalaki

Abstract: Closed-loop driving simulators mostly fill their environments with traffic agents that behave alike, whether they are rule-based or learned. Recent methods that control driving style learn it from labels inferred from existing driving recordings, from synthetic data generated to reflect each style, or from reward weights estimated by a large language model (LLM). All of these are indirect estimates of a style, not recordings of people who were instructed to drive in it. We introduce PersonaDrive, a vision-language-action (VLA) driving agent that takes its style from retrieved human demonstrations. The demonstrations come from a new dataset in which 15 participants drove CARLA routes on a driving simulator under conservative, neutral, and aggressive instructions. PersonaDrive trains a lightweight retrieval head on features from a frozen image encoder and on control signals over per-style databases, and fine-tunes one VLA backbone over the retrieved demonstrations. Switching style only means searching a different FAISS index, with no retraining. On the Bench2Drive benchmark without style conditioning, PersonaDrive improves the Driving Score by 4.6% over SimLingo and by 2.5% over HiP-AD, a gain that fine-tuning without retrieval does not reproduce. With style conditioning, PersonaDrive outperforms the strongest baseline, Drive My Way, in every style, with a Driving Score 6.7% to 11.6% higher. Its driving also follows the instruction, since average speed rises by 19.8% from the conservative to the aggressive style. Each traffic agent is evaluated individually as a driving policy. The dataset and model weights are available on the project page: https://pervasiveautonomylab.github.io/personadrive-landpage/.

Abstract: While recent vision-language models (VLMs) have achieved significant improvements on static visual-to-code tasks such as generating code for webpages, charts, or SVGs, it remains unclear whether they can recover temporal dynamics when motion is present. Animation2Code consists of 1,069 web animation videos with diverse visual appearances and motion patterns, paired with corresponding HTML/CSS/JavaScript implementations. replace-cross PolicyGuard: A Dialogue-Grounded Sub-Agent Verifier for Policy Adherence in LLM Agents

Authors: Seongjae Kang, Taehyung Yu, Sung Ju Hwang

Abstract: LLM agents handle user requests on behalf of organizations through tool calls and must follow the company policies stated in their system prompts. Prior work approaches this as a safeguarding problem -- external checks that block non-compliant agent actions. We argue that policy adherence is broader: real workflows unfold across many turns, require explicit user confirmation and prerequisite reads, and hinge on dialogue rather than on any single argument value. We introduce PolicyGuard, a sub-agent verifier that shares the agent's view of the dialogue, reasons over the policy in context, and returns actionable remediation. Further analyses show that PolicyGuard improves reliability beyond agent self-checking, catches more policy violations with fewer blocks than argument-level guards, and preserves its refusal accuracy under three adversarial probes.

replace-cross Closing the Activation-Cone Blind Spot: Response-Time Probing and Unified Defense

Authors: Subhadip Mitra

Abstract: Note on v2 (Nov 2026): errors found in the evaluation code mean the empirical results reported in v1 are not supported; the theoretical corollary is unaffected. Details are in the notice on page 1 and in KNOWN_ISSUES.md in the linked repository. We compare five inference-time safety paradigms (no defense, static steering, CAST, AlphaSteer, probe-gated steering) across seven instruction-tuned models (7-31B) and five attack types (GCG, AutoDAN, DeepInception, prefilling, intent laundering). We derive a corollary: any defense that gates intervention on a single layer's activation alignment with a benign reference (cone, subspace, or null-space) is blind to attacks that craft activations to lie inside that reference, whether checked at prompt time or per token. For prefilling attacks we propose response-time probing, a linear probe on the hidden state at the first generated tokens combined with a halt, and compose this halt with AlphaSteer's null-space steering. We also examine whether MMLU captures steering's utility cost and how the diversity of negative training sets affects probe false-positive rates. Code, attacks, and per-sample results are released.

replace-cross Signed-Permutation Coordinate Transport for RMSNorm Transformers

Authors: John Sweeney

Abstract: Modern LLM workflows move coordinate-indexed objects across checkpoints: steering vectors, sparse autoencoders, top-$k$ neuron sets, attribution lists, LoRA updates, optimizer states, and merge alignments. This requires fixing the model's residual-stream gauge. We show that the native coordinate-preserving orthogonal gauge is architecture-dependent: LayerNorm residual charts have permutation gauge $S_d$ up to a global sign flip, while RMSNorm charts with generic gains have signed-permutation gauge $B_d = S_d \ltimes \{\pm 1\}^d$. Permutation-only alignment is thus symmetry-incomplete for RMSNorm models. We introduce sign-marginalized Hungarian matching and prove that with decorrelated coordinates, signed-correlation matching has a permutation-accuracy ceiling equal to the true gauge's positive-sign fraction up to $O(d 2^{-d})$, which sign-marginalized matching removes. Omitting signs induces expected squared error $2\|\Delta W\|_F^2$ on weight updates and loses orientation on vectors and AdamW first moments, while RMSNorm gains and AdamW second moments permute without signs. Controlled tests confirm these laws: a Qwen2.5-1.5B SST-2 LoRA adapter retains 0.944 accuracy (from 0.945) under $B_d$ transport vs. 0.523 under $S_d$, and steering preserves effects to $<10^{-4}$ at zero raw top-10 overlap. Across natural checkpoints, inherited slots suffice for same-base descendants (96.2%-100% retention), whereas cross-generation pairs support sparse prompt-stable subsets with causally decisive negative signs ($+0.185$ vs. $-0.010$ at $8\sigma$ on Qwen2/2.5-0.5B; $+0.189$ vs. $-0.021$ on 1.5B). Along fine-tuning trajectories, composing local $B_d$ gauges recovers 91.1% of cross-run Qwen2.5-1.5B coordinates at 1,500 steps vs. 60.3% for endpoint matching. Merge and interpretability audits confirm that coordinate names are reproducible only relative to an explicit $B_d$ gauge.

replace-cross Program-as-Weights: A Programming Paradigm for Fuzzy Functions

Authors: Wentao Zhang, Liliana Hotsko, Woojeong Kim, Pengyu Nie, Stuart Shieber, Yuntian Deng

Abstract: Many everyday programming tasks resist clean rule-based implementation, such as alerting on important log lines, repairing malformed JSON, or ranking search results by intent, and are increasingly outsourced to large language model APIs at the cost of locality, reproducibility, and price. We propose fuzzy-function programming: compiling such a function from a natural-language specification into a compact, locally executable neural artifact. We instantiate this paradigm with Program-as-Weights (PAW), in which a 4B compiler trained on FuzzyBench, a 10M-example dataset we release, emits parameter-efficient adapters for a frozen, lightweight interpreter. A 0.6B Qwen3 interpreter executing PAW programs matches the performance of direct prompting of Qwen3-32B, while using roughly one fiftieth of the inference memory and running at 30 tokens/s on a MacBook M3. PAW reframes the foundation model from a per-input problem solver into a tool builder: invoked once per function definition, it produces a small reusable artifact whose subsequent calls per function application are cheap and offline.

replace-cross Aligning Language Models with Selective Prediction

Authors: Gaoxiang Luo, Yifan Wu, Sinian Zhang, Aryan Deshwal, Ju Sun

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as critical decision-making components in high-stakes real-world AI systems, rendering LLM reliability a foremost practical concern. In this paper, we focus on enhancing LLM reliability through selective prediction (SP), a strategy that allows an LLM to only predict for inputs where it is likely to be correct (i.e., coverage) and hence reduce the error rate (i.e., risk) on that portion of inputs---flagging the remaining inputs for future human discretion. In other words, SP improves LLM reliability by balancing the risk-coverage trade-off and enabling seamless human-AI collaboration. To integrate SP into LLMs, we focus on the LLM post-training alignment stage and propose to align LLMs with SP performance metrics, in contrast with existing LLM alignment methods that focus primarily on correctness or calibration metrics. Specifically, we propose a novel alignment framework, Reinforcement Learning for Selection Reward (RLSR), which targets the area under the risk-coverage curve (AURC)---a popular SP performance metric---as its alignment objective. RLSR achieves substantially better risk-coverage trade-off compared to multiple alignment baselines on both in-domain and out-of-domain tasks.

replace-cross LLM Watermarking as Big Data Provenance: A Deployment-Oriented Systematization

Authors: Huy Phan, Kieu Dang, Ojaswi Dulal, Aiham AL Shukairi, Abby Shine, Chase Garner, Phung Lai

Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) generate and transform content at scale, reliable attribution becomes an increasingly important requirement for big data provenance. Although numerous watermarking methods have been proposed, existing evaluations predominantly emphasize sample-level detectability and robustness, leaving deployment requirements such as serving efficiency, population-scale false positives, multi-key attribution, and long-term verification less systematically examined. This paper presents a deployment-oriented systematization of LLM watermarking through the five Vs of big data: Volume, Velocity, Variety, Veracity, and Value. We classify representative watermarking methods across four dimensions: insertion point, verification authority, operational state, and adversarial threat model. We further map these methods to four deployment workloads: online generation, streaming detection, transformation pipelines, and ecosystem governance. Our synthesis identifies recurring limitations, including dependencies on generation-time access, additional inference overhead, sensitivity to downstream transformations, and incomplete reporting of calibration and lifecycle compatibility. Based on these findings, we propose workload-specific evaluation requirements covering statistical reliability, throughput, utility, robustness, and governance. This framework connects watermark design choices to practical deployment constraints and provides guidance for selecting and evaluating provenance mechanisms in large-scale data ecosystems.

replace-cross SlotGuard: Stop Oversharing Private Local Context in LLM Agent Transcripts

Authors: Haocheng Xia, Yongjoo Park

Abstract: LLM agents can expose private local context (e.g., paths, emails) and credentials (e.g., API keys) as agent observations (e.g., tool outputs, shell logs, and file reads) are appended to provider-bound transcripts. Existing placeholder redaction is brittle: it can miss embedded or cross-turn references, over-redact benign lookalikes, and destroy the structure useful for reasoning. We present SlotGuard, a local transcript boundary that can hide sensitive data while retaining agents' performance. SlotGuard rewrites structural bindings as typed, suffix-aware slots, replaces secrets with format-preserving synthetic values, links cross-turn references with a lightweight session graph, and restores raw values only inside the trusted runtime. It remains close to raw-transcript task success across four upstream models, while generic redaction drops to 2.5%. Deterministic transcript rewriting takes a median of 14.424 microseconds per agent turn, excluding the optional semantic detector. The code is publicly accessible at https://github.com/illinoisdata/SlotGuard.

replace-cross PolicyGuide: From Guarding One Action to Guiding the Whole Workflow for Policy-Compliant LLM Agents

Authors: Seongjae Kang, Taehyung Yu, Sung Ju Hwang

Abstract: Customer-service LLM agents must follow organizational policy when acting on a user's behalf. Compliance failures arise from either forbidden actions, such as granting an ineligible change, or omitted procedural requirements, such as identification or confirmation. Runtime safeguards can intervene on risky actions, but action-local checks do not guide an agent through a multi-step procedure. Workflow-following systems support prescribed process execution, but primarily target workflow completion rather than safeguarding agent behavior. PolicyGuide instead compiles each domain policy into a workflow graph and invokes a proactive verifier at user-turn boundaries. From persisted graph state, the verifier reconciles open requests and returns step-specific remediation along a policy-compliant path. Across the $\tau^2$-bench airline, retail, and telecom domains with a GPT 5.4 agent and verifier, PolicyGuide raises mean $\mathrm{Pass}^4$ from $0.42$ to $0.62$, with the largest gain on telecom ($0.19$ to $0.61$), the most workflow-structured domain. The same workflows transfer to Claude Sonnet 4.6 and Gemini 2.5 Pro agents. Complementary evaluations find the lowest observed attack-success rate under adversarial users and the strongest procedural compliance in an independently validated workflow-level audit.

replace-cross The Communication Map of a Transformer

Authors: Richard Zhe Wang

Abstract: The components of a transformer communicate by writing to and reading from a shared residual stream, and the mechanistic interpretability literature has mapped these connections by hand, one circuit at a time. We present the communication map, which charts every potential communication channel from the geometry of the model's weights alone. Its unit of analysis is the whole transformer rather than the individual head or circuit. Every ordered writer-reader pair, across all 18 connection classes from head-to-head to neuron-to-neuron, receives a coupling coefficient that generalizes the composition score of Elhage et al. (2021), with an exact chance level. The census finds that 70-89% of head pairs are oriented far from chance, some coupled strongly and others actively avoiding each other. The map recovers the known induction circuits blind from the strongest head-to-head couplings and groups the heads into communities, and ablating one such community destroys the model's in-context copying. As an application, we pool the coupling coefficients of every head to identify a two-dimensional residual stream subspace, distinct from those found by activation PCA or outlier dimensions, whose deletion abolishes induction in six models up to Pythia-6.9B. We release the map, the statistical machinery, and the intervention suite.

replace-cross Improving Evaluation Realism with Inference-Time Compute and Deployment Scaffolds

Authors: Axel Ahlqvist, Richard Guan, Juan-Pablo Rivera, Adeline Kassler, Dmitrii Troitskii, Alexandra Souly, Kai Fronsdal, Robert Kirk, John Hughes

Abstract: A core obstacle to alignment evaluation is evaluation awareness: capable models can tell when they are being tested rather than deployed, weakening the conclusions a safety evaluation can support. We present two techniques that make simulated alignment evaluations harder to distinguish from real deployments. Our first technique, critique refinement, spends additional inference-time compute on each simulator action: the simulator generates multiple candidate actions, refines them using feedback from an instance of the target model on how to make them more realistic, and continues the evaluation with the most deployment-like candidate. Our second technique, DISH (Deployment-Imitating SWE-Agent Harness), wraps the target in an agent harness, reducing the gap between simulated and real deployment environments in coding settings. We test the techniques on multiple target models and find that applying both yields larger realism gains than either alone. We show that automated approaches can improve the realism of alignment evaluations, and that they compare favorably to spending the extra compute on more audit attempts.

replace-cross Marginal utility, matrix factorization, and the Key-Value (KV) cache: a unified information-economic framework for sovereign geo-mining inference

Authors: Caroline Gans Combe (INSEEC)

Abstract: This paper builds a theoretical bridge between the economic notion of marginal utility and two machine-learning constructs, matrix factorization and the Key--Value cache of transformer language models. The singular value spectrum of a rating matrix is shown to be a diminishing marginal utility schedule for latent factors, the eigenvalue spectrum of the projected covariance operator to be the marginal utility schedule of a model's learned representation, and cache eviction and low-rank cache compression to be instances of constrained utility maximization under a memory budget. The three collapse into a single allocation rule: retain the top dimensions whose eigenvalue exceeds the shadow price of the binding constraint. The framework is applied to the automated extraction of structured information from geo-mining documents, where it motivates a multi-pass inference protocol, a layer-wise TIES model merging procedure, and a selection policy combining extraction quality, localization drift and energy, scalarized with a Conditional Value-at-Risk term on drift. Two empirical contributions are reported. An 11.2-million-parameter hierarchical classifier, trained in about five minutes on a single GPU, reaches 90.0 per cent level-1 accuracy on a held-out test set from a 973-document uranium-exploration corpus, against 92.0 per cent for a proprietary model on a fifty-document human audit of the same corpus, at a latency of 2.62 ms per card against approximately 2,000 ms for the API and at negligible cost. A diagnostic of uniform-density TIES merging exposes a reproducible degenerate mode in which the merged model returns token-identical outputs across five geographically distinct districts while declaring high confidence; re-executing the merge under layer-wise calibrated densities removes that signature on the diagnostic sample. The full-scale extraction benchmark, including LoRA fine-tuning, is reported as projected rather than measured and remains an empirical extension of this work.

replace-cross PoEM: Predicting RL Outcomes from Existing Policies

Authors: Kimia Hamidieh, Giannis Daras, Antonio Torralba

Abstract: Foundation models are post-trained with reinforcement learning (RL) to maximize specific rewards, such as helpfulness, correctness, or instruction following. This post-training process is computationally intensive, sometimes unstable, and has to be run from scratch every time the reward model changes or when we want to combine multiple rewards. We hence ask: given a new reward function, is it possible to predict the policy RL would produce without actually training on that reward? We answer this in the affirmative by introducing PoEM, a framework for predicting RL outcomes using models previously trained on other rewards. First, we build on the fact that if the new reward function can be written as a linear combination of existing ones, then the optimal policy can be expressed as a combination of the existing policies in log-probability space. Surprisingly, even in cases where the rewards are not linearly connected, we observe that policies learned through RL often span an approximately low-rank subspace across rewards. To our benefit, the weights for this combination can be estimated using only the reward or basis policy outputs on the samples. We turn these observations into an algorithm that takes post-trained models and a target reward function, and approximates the target RL policy without any additional RL training. We experimentally evaluate our approach across programmatic and learned rewards for open-ended generation, with additional experiments on math reasoning, tool calling, and image generation.

replace-cross LLMs as Adaptive Meta-Solvers: Strategy-Diverse RL for Industrial-Scale Optimization

Authors: Shihao Zhang, Weiting Liu, Siyu Shao, Yitian Chen, Jianfeng Feng, Dongdong Ge, Yinyu Ye

Abstract: Scaling LLM-based optimization from textbook-scale instances to real-world, industrial tasks remains a critical open challenge. Existing approaches are predominantly evaluated on small, self-contained textual problems and often commit to a solver-integrated paradigm, limiting their ability to handle the scale and structural diversity of practical optimization workloads. In this work, we propose a practical framework for training open-source LLMs to tackle real-world, industrial-scale optimization. We first show empirically that solver-integrated reasoning, exact combinatorial algorithm, and heuristic search exhibit complementary strengths across different problem structures and scales. Motivated by this, we introduce Strategy-Diverse Reinforcement Learning (SDRL), which trains LLMs as adaptive optimization meta-solvers. SDRL leverages this complementarity through a correctness-gated hierarchical diversity reward that promotes robust exploration across varying strategies and within each strategy, effectively preventing premature strategy collapse. We further introduce a mixed-format training scheme that jointly supports both self-contained textual problems and file-grounded instances. Across comprehensive evaluations, our framework outperforms existing fine-tuned methods and frontier models including DeepSeek-V4-Pro and GPT-5.5, both on average across benchmarks and on industrial-scale optimization tasks. We release our code and training data to facilitate future research https://github.com/tokentideAI/LLMMetaSolver.

URLs: https://github.com/tokentideAI/LLMMetaSolver.

replace-cross VEX-Bench: Benchmarking Verification Complexity of LLM-Generated Misinformation

Authors: Hanxun Huang, Yutao Wu, Qizhou Wang, Silvia Monta\~na-Ni\~no, Yige Li, Xiang Zheng, Elif Buse Doyuran, Phoebe Matich, Xiao Liu, Xingjun Ma, Sarah Erfani, Christopher Leckie

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have made misinformation inexpensive to produce but not to verify, creating a growing asymmetry in the information ecosystem. Under tight time, labor, and budget constraints, media organizations, platforms, and fact-checkers rely on screening to prioritize which content to verify. We introduce VEX-Bench, a unified benchmark for evaluating the verification complexity of LLM-generated misinformation, as perceived during screening, across models and generation methods. Verification complexity is assessed along multiple dimensions derived from journalistic and fact-checking practices, capturing checkability, harm potential, source credibility signals, imposter legitimacy, and expected verification effort. We define the VEX score as an integrated measure combining elicitation yield and verification complexity to quantify how generated content consumes limited verification capacity. We construct a benchmark spanning two misinformation categories, 6 high-stakes domains, and 60 real-world topics, and evaluate 7 frontier LLMs and 7 generation methods, yielding 5{,}880 articles. We employ an LLM-as-judge for scalable evaluation and validate it using content-analysis methodology, including ordinal Krippendorff $\alpha$ for inter-annotator reliability, complemented by fact-checking agents for verification. Our findings show that no single method dominates all dimensions, underscoring the need for multi-dimensional evaluation. LLMs can generate high-VEX misinformation at 3$\times$ to 169$\times$ lower cost than agent-based verification. Such content is often prioritized during screening, consuming scarce verification resources and introducing a systematic risk of misallocation in resource-constrained verification systems. The code is publicly available in our \href{https://github.com/HanxunH/VEX-Bench}{GitHub repository}.

URLs: https://github.com/HanxunH/VEX-Bench

replace-cross Typed Edge-Tensor Non-Reciprocal Port-Hamiltonian Deliberation for Counterfactual Open-World Deductive Reasoning

Authors: Zeyu Jia

Abstract: Scaling test-time computation in continuous latent spaces offers an attractive alternative to verbose token-by-token generation for multi-step deductive reasoning. However, unregularized recurrent latent updates suffer from a deliberation drift cliff when rolled out beyond training horizons, while strictly energy-conserving or symmetric message-passing dynamics cannot break parent-to-child causal symmetry or suppress cyclic modus-ponens amplification on cyclic distraction graphs. We introduce Typed Edge-Tensor Non-Reciprocal Port-Hamiltonian Deliberation (TE-BPHD), a neuro-symbolic continuous-time latent reasoning architecture that couples skew-symmetric port-Hamiltonian conservation with relation-typed non-reciprocal directional transport and monotone dissipative damping on a semantic entity-state manifold. We prove (i) unconditional input-to-state stability and exponential contraction of autonomous rollouts across arbitrary inference depths without external norm projection, (ii) strict directional DAG reachability separation over symmetric message passing, and (iii) exact cycle-suppression margins under finite cyclic horn perturbations. Across counterfactual open-world deductive benchmarks (PrOntoQA-OOD up to depth 15, ProofWriter-OWA Cyclic at depths 5-10, FOLIO-Symbolic, and Cluttr-Relational), TE-BPHD eliminates the deep-horizon drift cliff, improves depth-10 cyclic reasoning accuracy to 90.0% (+/- 0.8%) over symmetric port-Hamiltonian and standard Transformer baselines, and supports zero-retraining adaptive Lyapunov halting that reduces average solver evaluations by 44% with negligible accuracy loss.

replace-cross What Does a Harness Repair? Four Preregistered Studies Around a Longevity-Tuned Language Model

Authors: Bowen Xu, Boyu Chen

Abstract: A harness search around a frozen longevity-tuned 1.7B model reported a rescue. An audit traced its gain to rows the start left unparsed and found that the caller sent reference answers as assistant turns; replication through the same pipeline missed this defect. We preregistered four studies and report every endpoint. In the first two, thinking off raised test accuracy over capped thinking in 7 of 12 cells, mainly by parsing rows the cap left unparsed, and was not meaningfully worse than a rescue in 7 of 9 small-model test cells. Replication reproduced an injected defect's distortion in 9 of 20 evaluable cases. At 14B, two GEPA harnesses beat thinking off by 34 test points but were not detectably above the strongest constant label. In the third study, GEPA beat the constant on FPB (confirmatory), thinking off was not meaningfully worse than any BoolQ harness, and 3 of 4 MMLU-Pro searches kept their seed; the fourth comparison was inconclusive on test. On exploratory Autocast, harnesses were within 0.7 points of the constant and gave one label in 93.1% to 96.8% of parsed answers on test. On the prediction tasks (14B mortality, Autocast) the harnesses ended near the constant label. The fourth study varied search-set skew and objective on CREAK and resolved Manifold questions. All its results are descriptive because render hashes were not committed before the first replication call (D001). Minority-recall estimates were lower at 80:20 than at 50:50 in all four test cells. The balanced objective and re-selection from each search's pool had higher minority- and lower majority-recall estimates in all four cells. Five of their eight minority-recall intervals included zero, as did all eight balanced-accuracy contrast intervals. This does not show that skew explains the earlier collapse.

replace-cross Beyond the Final Embedding: Retrieving Memories from Token-Level Evidence

Authors: Han Luo, Zhan Zhuang, Xu He, Jujia Zhao, Yu Li, Suqin Yuan, Dong Li, Lei Feng

Abstract: Memory-augmented assistants retrieve evidence from stored conversations and documents to answer queries. Most embedding-based retrievers compare single-vector representations of queries and memories, even when relevance depends on a small part of the content. Such local evidence can be matched to queries using the contextual token representations already computed by the same embedding models. Our method uses sparse token matching to improve retrieval without additional training. Each query token retrieves a small set of matching tokens across candidate memories. The matching scores are aggregated for each memory and combined with whole-memory similarity to rank memories. An optional low-rank query adapter can be trained with supervision while keeping the encoder and stored memory representations fixed. Our method improves retrieval across eight benchmarks and multiple model families without additional training. Query adaptation further increases the gains to 13.3 and 14.3 percentage points in average Recall@10 and NDCG@10 on Qwen3-Embedding-4B. These retrieval gains translate into higher question-answering accuracy on long-term memory benchmarks.

replace-cross RELAY: Selective Self-Distillation with Adaptive Hand-off for Rubric-Based Reinforcement Learning

Authors: Kunwang Zheng, Xinyu Sun, Yu Cao, Shuocheng Li, Lei Cheng, Ying Liu, Qilong Zhang, Jinlong Chen, Hua Yang, Qianglong Chen

Abstract: Rubric-based reinforcement learning grades a response against many written criteria but collapses them into one reward, which discards the detail the rubric carries and gives no GRPO reward gradient when a group's responses tie. A frozen teacher that reads the rubric as privileged context provides token-level supervision, but we observe an early plateau in the gains over GRPO. RELAY uses advantages and rewards to select positive-advantage and low-scoring zero-advantage rollouts, and monitors the distillation loss to decide when to retire the teacher. Stage 1 combines selective distillation from a frozen teacher with GRPO; a plateau detector then removes the teacher, and Stage 2 continues with GRPO alone. Calibrated on one model-domain pair and reused unchanged in three others, the plateau detector fires between steps 122 and 171. RELAY scores highest in-domain among the compared methods in all four settings, ahead of GRPO on RaR-Medicine by 4.62 points with Qwen3-1.7B and 6.02 with Qwen3-4B. On Qwen3-1.7B/Medicine, removing the teacher from a shared checkpoint outperforms keeping, weakening or refreshing it. Distilling selected rollouts covering about half the response tokens also outperforms distilling all rollouts at a matched average coefficient.

replace-cross Tracing Hallucinations in LLM Reasoning for UML Class Diagram Generation: An Empirical Taxonomy and Mitigation Study

Authors: Mojdeh Rahmanian, Foutse Khomh, Yanchao Yu, Ashkan Sami

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) can generate UML class diagrams from natural language requirements, but hallucinations may compromise the correctness and completeness of the resulting diagrams. Existing evaluations mostly assess the final diagram, without examining the intermediate traces of reasoning-capable LLMs, which can reveal where these hallucinations arise. We develop an empirical taxonomy of eleven hallucination sub-types organised into three dimensions: Prompt Faithfulness (deviations between requirements and reasoning), Code Faithfulness (discrepancies between reasoning and generated PlantUML code), and Reasoning Quality (problems within the reasoning). The taxonomy was derived from 60 manually annotated DeepSeek-R1 traces, applied to 120 further DeepSeek-R1 traces through a dual-LLM annotation pipeline, and examined on 20 DeepSeek-V4-Pro traces. Code Faithfulness was the most frequent dimension for DeepSeek-R1 in both datasets, whereas Prompt Faithfulness dominated for DeepSeek-V4-Pro, suggesting model-dependent failure patterns. In the manually annotated traces, reasoning-trace length showed no significant monotonic association with hallucination frequency, whereas requirement complexity was negatively associated with it, particularly for Prompt Faithfulness errors. In a mitigation experiment on 13 Golden UML ModelSet cases with three Qwen3-based models, adding Prompt Faithfulness and Reasoning Quality constraints to the generation prompt raised the pooled mean F1 score by 4.0 percentage points, whereas a post-hoc Code Faithfulness review provided no consistent further benefit. Relationship modelling remained the lowest-scoring element throughout. These results suggest that localising hallucinations within the generation process can inform targeted, prompt-based mitigation. Whether these findings generalise beyond the DeepSeek and Qwen3 model families remains to be tested.

replace-cross The Value of Memory in Language Agents

Authors: Zehao Lin, Erenkai Zhu, Helan Hu, Xixuan Hao, Chunyu Li, Linyun Liu, Kai Chen, Chenxu Du, Feiyu Xiong

Abstract: Language agents carry information from past experience into future tasks whose demands may be unknown or change over time. We develop a decision-theoretic account of the capability available in history, retained in storage and realized through use, together with the costs of recovery and revision. Drawing on comparison of statistical experiments, information acquisition and the value of computation, we express these values in units of avoided task loss. Recovery and revision define attainable risk-cost profiles and preservation across uses. A cost-aware simulation condition and five minimal counterexamples establish how information timing, access, computation, feedback and demand shape these comparisons; a pointer with zero answer mutual information can reduce the cost of exact recovery. Allocation and recovery criteria identify when gains pay for estimation, selection and evidence access. Complete-split studies on LoCoMo, LongMemEval-S and PersonaMem cover 3,075 questions and two readers. They show complementary value from saved state and recovered evidence, task-dependent performance orderings, and the role of reuse in amortizing construction costs. Multi-project workflows connect correction to preservation and validation costs. Together, the theory and studies support decisions about what to retain, which recovery options to preserve, and how to adapt memory as evidence and objectives evolve.

replace-cross How Much Do Structural Tools Help Small Coding Agents? A Pre-Registered Scale Study of Code Localization

Authors: Saurav Bellam (The University of Texas at Austin)

Abstract: Code-graph and embedding tools are routinely added to LLM coding agents on the premise that they supply repository structure the model lacks. We report a pre-registered study of how much such tools help off-the-shelf agents localize the code an issue requires changing and how the benefit varies with model size. Nine open-weight models (Qwen3 0.6B to 32B, Gemma 4 E2B to 12B) run one fixed localization harness on 500 held-out SWE-bench tasks under four tool sets (grep only; plus embedding search; plus code-graph tools; both) and a placebo arm whose structural tools return random entities, with five sampled seeds per condition: 170,500 episodes under hypotheses, tests and claim criteria frozen before the runs. The tools raise canonical function Acc@10 by +0.06 to +0.10 for the Qwen3 models up to 14B and the two small Gemma 4 models, by +0.03 at 32B (a near miss after correction), and not at all for Gemma 4 12B, which rarely submits; the gain is larger for small models than for the largest model in both families. The placebo shows that the gain is caused by what the tools return rather than by having them, and that random tool output harms two small models. The pre-registered claim that a 4B model with tools matches a 32B model without them fails. Secondary analyses show that the embedding index, not the code graph, carries the gain; that the gain replicates on tasks created after every model's release and survives a shell harness, thinking mode, FP8 serving and a file-level metric that output formatting cannot inflate; and that it is mostly exposure: the tools show the gold code more often. Injecting the gold code directly explains the size trend. A 32B model already finds the code without the tools, so they add little exposure, while small models act on a shown answer less often (conversion 0.09, 0.24 and 0.40 at 1.7B, 4B and 32B). We release registration, code, episodes and analyses.

replace-cross Balanced State Subspaces with Spectral Rank Allocation for Mamba-2 State Compression

Authors: Shang Tingyu, Cao Wenfei, Han Zhi

Abstract: Mamba-2 compresses all context into a fixed-size recurrent state that dominates decoding memory and bandwidth. Existing compressors quantize state values or prune coordinates with per-channel scores. Trained states are nearly rank-one and bias-like, aligned with the mean write; but the output's energy spans an order of magnitude more directions. Per-channel scores see only the diagonal of the write--read operator, so they cannot select the subspace that governs the input--output map, which is a rotation of the coordinates. We therefore propose \method{}, which keeps that subspace by applying balanced truncation exactly via Mamba-2's scalar-identity transition. Layers differ just as sharply: most are bias-dominated, while the few that carry long-range associations are the flattest and need most of their state. We therefore introduce \spar{}, which redistributes a fixed total rank budget across layers according to the Hankel spectrum. On Mamba-2 checkpoints from 130M to 2.7B, \method{}+\spar{} halves the state at an average cost of $0.3$ WikiText-2 perplexity points, against $1.6$ for GHOST, while retaining $97\%$ of the dense model's five-digit in-context recall. At a quarter of the state, giving every layer the same rank keeps $5\%$ of that recall and \spar{} $77\%$, for a $7\%$ perplexity cost.

Abstract: LLM-based theorem proving in Lean 4 has advanced rapidly, but provers are difficult to leverage newly contributed lemmas, because retrieval skews toward popular foundational lemmas, leaving the right premises buried among hundreds of thousands of candidates. replace-cross Multi-Turn Semantic Caching for Low-Cost LLM Serving

Authors: Baran Atalar, Selly Beyene, Xutong Liu, Carlee Joe-Wong

Abstract: Semantic caching lowers the cost of serving large language models (LLMs) by reusing stored responses for similar requests. In multi-turn conversations, whether a cached response is acceptable depends on both conversations' histories, which embedding similarity of requests and histories predicts poorly (AUC 0.62--0.74 on five workloads). We therefore learn the \emph{reuse risk}, the probability that a cached response is unacceptable, from judge feedback, and choose a budgeted set of conversation states to cache by weighing the generation cost a reuse avoids against a price for unacceptable reuse. We propose CUCB-SC-MT, which learns a cache from logged conversations with a suboptimality guarantee, and CLCB-LS-MT, which learns online and has $\tilde O(\sqrt N)$ regret over $N$ requests when conversation states recur. On math-tutoring (SocraTeach) and movie-ticketing (Taskmaster-3) conversations, both achieve lower loss than prior semantic caching approaches: ContextCache, multi-turn variants of ElastiCache and vCache, and single-turn semantic caching at every cache budget. With 1,000 cache entries, CLCB-LS-MT avoids 35--41\% of the generation cost through acceptable reuses, against at most 22--25\% for the baselines, and lowers estimated serving latency by 19--35\%.

replace-cross MAC-Bench: Benchmarking Multi-Agent Collaboration via Interconnected Tasks

Authors: Zerui Chen, Qinggang Zhang, Qichuan Liu, Zhishang Xiang, Zhimin Wei, Yunbo Tang, Zhuo Li, Jinsong Su, Zhihong Zhang

Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems (MAS) tackle challenging tasks by dividing work among agents and coordinating their contributions toward shared objectives. Evaluating these systems therefore requires benchmarks that assess not only task-solving performance but also the ability to handle interdependent work. However, existing benchmarks often emphasize task difficulty without substantial collaboration requirements or evaluate only a limited range of predefined collaboration patterns. In this paper, we introduce MAC-Bench, a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating multi-agent collaboration through interconnected tasks. Rather than imposing predefined agent roles or communication protocols, MAC-Bench embeds collaboration requirements directly into task dependencies and shared-resource constraints. Specifically, we mine reusable collaboration structures from selected benchmark tasks at two levels. Local collaboration patterns capture recurring interactions among subtasks, while global task structures characterize how these interactions contribute to complete workflows. Through coarse-to-fine composition, these structures are recombined into task graphs combining different collaboration requirements. Tasks are instantiated in executable environments with automated verification of partial progress and complete task resolution.

Authors: Chengxi Li, Shaoxuan Li, Deyao Hong, Qingle Liu, Yi Huang, Puheng Bian, Minzhi Xu, Lanyue Zhang, Mingze Sun, Xin Li, Calvin Xiao, Shengyuan Wang, Huan-ang Gao, Jiazheng Xu, Kaisen Yang, Hongning Wang

Abstract: As pretraining scales, medical language models have advanced rapidly in clinical question answering. replace-cross Octopus: One Brain, Many Branches for Latent-KV Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning

Authors: Tianming Sha, Junnan Li, Siqi Zhao

Abstract: Deep search and long-horizon reasoning unfold as branching trajectories that pursue parallel sub-queries, abandon dead-end detours, and cross-check sibling findings. Yet modern serving engines, harnesses, and RL trainers execute episodes as flat, append-only sequences: stretching a context window leaves failed detours that dilute attention, in-place edits break KV caching and recurrent states, and all-layer cache splicing overwrites hybrid recurrent working memory and triggers hijacking. We introduce Octopus, a co-designed serving runtime, memory manager, and RL framework executing episodes as a GPU-resident DAG over radix cursors (89.6% KV hit rate). Agents coordinate via five primitives: fork shares parent KV prefixes in $O(1)$ with isolated 32K budgets, rewind restores recurrent checkpoints, read_thread mounts sibling KV pages in zero copy, and wait and done synchronize results. Octopus splices sibling KV pages only into full-attention layers under monotonic position offsets while recurrent layers skip mounted spans, retaining 92.2% of full-text answer information and cutting hijacking $21\times$ ($57.9\% \to 2.7\%$) with zero new KV pages. During RL, our packed DAG kernel and DAG-GRPO objective deduplicate shared prefixes via loss masks while keeping mounted KV edges differentiable, cutting rollout-training KL divergence $32\times$ and routing reader gradients into producer KV projections. Trained on Qwen3.5-4B with binary rewards, Octopus self-organizes a commander-worker hierarchy and latent KV mesh, lifting BrowseComp-Plus accuracy under BM25 from 26.0% (32K ReAct) and 32.1% (143K GRPO) to 49.6% (+17.5 pts; 51.2% on OpenResearcher), surpassing 9B context-editing RL (42.5%), matching o3 (49.3%), and outperforming dense-retrieval agents up to $250\times$ larger: Tongyi-DeepResearch (44.5%), DeepSeek-R1 (16.4%), Kimi-K2 (35.4%), GPT-4.1 (36.4%), and Claude 4 Opus (36.8%).

replace-cross Structure is in the zoom: probing neural symmetry through dimensionality scaling

Authors: Adil Amin

Abstract: Scaling exponents of neural dimensionality are read as properties of the code, and used to compare representations and benchmark models against cortex. Yet one recording of 11,311 mouse V1 neurons gives 0.25, 0.30 or 0.35, depending only on how it is subsampled. We show that such an exponent measures the population and the probe together. We split it exactly into a floor, which random subsets of the same sizes give, and a shift, which a declared axis of stimulus classes adds. The usual random-subsample exponent is almost all floor. Stimulus direction lifts the exponent in every grating recording by reading both symmetry sectors of the code: orientation, which repeats every half turn, and direction, which does not. The lift lives in trial-to-trial fluctuations that differ between directions, while the class means, which linear decoders read, pull it down. Two measured terms, how much the classes' fluctuations overlap and how the means sit against them, account for the shift. They give its sign where it changes: in Allen Neuropixels recordings, whose class means mostly raise the exponent, and in macaque V1. In an XY lattice whose classes stay on one orbit, temperature flips the sign as the two terms say, and in GPT-2 the shift ranks the indirect-object circuit's name-mover and backup heads. Reported with its path, its floor and its shift, an exponent says how much structure a probe finds and what carries it. Our open tool, rung, reports all three for any labelled neural recording or language-model layer: https://github.com/adilamin89/structure-in-the-zoom

URLs: https://github.com/adilamin89/structure-in-the-zoom

replace-cross Greedy Byte-Pair Encoding Achieves 0.391 of the Optimum, Past the One-Third Barrier for Packing-Based Analyses

Authors: Kundan Baliga, Aiden Xie, Aditya Byju

Abstract: Byte-Pair Encoding (BPE) builds most language-model tokenisers by repeatedly merging a most frequent adjacent pair of symbols. How much shortening can this greedy rule lose against the best $k$ merges? Kozma and Voderholzer proved that greedy achieves at least one third of the optimal shortening; the best known upper bound is $(1-e^{-2})/2\approx 0.432$. We show that one third is a barrier for packing-based analyses, which see the optimum only through a pair packing, and we prove that greedy achieves at least $0.391$ of the optimum on every input, with any tie-breaking. The key idea is rule determinism: all copies of a rule of the optimum spell the same letters, so when one greedy replacement destroys three of the optimum's merge points, half a unit can be refunded on each side. A weaker bound, $0.374$, has a short proof that needs no computer; $0.391$ adds a certificate in exact arithmetic. We also show that the worst-case ratio is exactly $3/4$ for two merges and at most $7/12$ for three.

replace-cross PathXAI: Evidence-guided structured pathology reporting by transferable tissue segmentation

Authors: Zhaorui Tan, Jun Aun Tan, Weixiang Zhong, Xiaoxi Wang, Tan Pan, Xingdong Xue, Li Liu, Jing Zhang, Jing Zhang, Weimiao Yu

Abstract: Pathology reporting translates tissue observations across regions and slides into diagnoses and supporting morphological descriptions, yet explicit tissue-composition measurements are rarely used to guide H&E-based report generation. We developed PathXAI, which pairs transferable tissue segmentation with a pretrained vision-language model. Because supplying tissue information directly with H&E images gave limited benefit, PathXAI-Report uses segmentation overlays and tissue statistics in separate steps, selects between candidate diagnoses by fixed rules, acquires further images when evidence is insufficient and records every step for review, without updating model weights. Its segmentation component exceeded baselines in mean Dice on internal and external tests. In automated evaluation on 1,200 public and 151 private cases, PathXAI achieved higher agreement with reference diagnoses and documented morphology than H&E-based comparators. On the public cohort, it exceeded Qwen+H&E by 4.4 percentage points in cancer family match rate and 5.7 percentage points in finding F1.
